for a discussion of notions of optimality in MDPs.

5.1
Pareto Optimality

In this section we show that
Pareto optimality is not a useful criterion for optimality
since for any environment class containing
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{\mathrm{CCM}}
,
all policies are Pareto optimal.

Definition 5.1

(Pareto Optimality;
Hutter 2005
, Def. 5.22
)
.

A policy
\pi
is

Pareto optimal in the set of environments
\mathcal{M}
iff
there is no policy
\tilde{\pi}
such that

V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\nu}(\epsilon)\geq V^{\pi}_{\nu}(\epsilon)

for all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}
and

V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\rho}(\epsilon)>V^{\pi}_{\rho}(\epsilon)

for at least one
\rho\in\mathcal{M}
.

The literature provides the following result.

Theorem 5.2

(AIXI is Pareto Optimal;
Hutter 2002a
, Thm. 2
)
.

Every
\xi
-optimal policy is Pareto optimal in
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
.

The following theorem was proved for deterministic policies
in
Leike and Hutter 2015c
.
Here we extend it to stochastic policies.

Theorem 5.3

(Pareto Optimality is Trivial)
.

Every policy is Pareto optimal in any class
\mathcal{M}\supseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{\mathrm{CCM}}
.

The proof proceeds as follows:
for a given policy
\pi
, we construct a set of ‘buddy environments’
that reward
\pi
and punish other policies.
Together they can defend against any policy
\tilde{\pi}

that tries to take the crown of Pareto optimality from
\pi
.

Proof.

We assume
(0,0)
and
(0,1)\in\mathcal{E}
.
Moreover, assume there is a policy
\pi
that is not Pareto optimal.
Then there is a policy
\tilde{\pi}
that
Pareto dominates

\pi
, i.e.,

V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\rho}(\epsilon)>V^{\pi}_{\rho}(\epsilon)

for some
\rho\in\mathcal{M}
, and

V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\nu}(\epsilon)\geq V^{\pi}_{\nu}(\epsilon)
for all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}
.
From
V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\rho}(\epsilon)>V^{\pi}_{\rho}(\epsilon)
and

Section 4.2.2
we get that
there is a shortest and lexicographically first history

\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime}
consistent with
\pi
and
\tilde{\pi}
such that

\pi(\alpha\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})>\tilde{\pi}(\alpha\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})

for some action
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}

and
V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})>V^{\pi}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})
.
Consequently there is an
i\geq k
such that
\gamma_{i}>0
,
and hence
\Gamma_{k}>0
.
We define the environment
\mu

that first reproduces the separating history
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime}

and then, if
\alpha
is the next action, returns reward
1
forever, and
otherwise returns reward
0
forever.
Formally,
\mu
is defined by

\mu(e_{1:t}\mid e_{<t}\parallel a_{1:t}):=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }t<k\text{ and }e_{t}=e^{\prime}_{t},\\
1,&\text{if }t\geq k\text{ and }a_{k}=\alpha\text{ and }r_{t}=1\text{ and }o_{t}=0,\\
1,&\text{if }t\geq k\text{ and }a_{k}\neq\alpha\text{ and }r_{t}=0=o_{t},\text{ and}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The environment
\mu
is computable,
even if the policy
\pi
is not:
for a fixed history
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}^{\prime}
and action
\alpha
,
there exists a program computing
\mu
;
therefore
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{\mathrm{CCM}}
.
We get the following value difference for the policies
\pi
and
\tilde{\pi}
:

\displaystyle V^{\pi}_{\mu}(\epsilon)-V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\mu}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{\pi}_{\mu}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\gamma_{t}r_{t}+\sum_{t=k}^{\infty}\gamma_{t}r_{t}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\mu}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\gamma_{t}r_{t}-\sum_{t=k}^{\infty}\gamma_{t}r_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\pi(\alpha\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})\sum_{t=k}^{\infty}\gamma_{t}-\tilde{\pi}(\alpha\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})\sum_{t=k}^{\infty}\gamma_{t}\right)\mu^{\pi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\big(\pi(\alpha\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})-\tilde{\pi}(\alpha\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})\big)\mu^{\pi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}^{\prime})\Gamma_{k}>0

Hence
V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\mu}(\epsilon)<V^{\pi}_{\mu}(\epsilon)
,
which contradicts the fact that
\tilde{\pi}
Pareto dominates
\pi
since

\mathcal{M}\supseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{\mathrm{CCM}}\ni\mu
.
∎

Note that the environment
\mu
we defined
in the proof of
Section 5.1

is actually just a finite-state POMDP,
so Pareto optimality is also trivial for smaller environment classes.

5.2
Bad Priors

In this section we give three examples of universal priors
that cause a AIXI to misbehave drastically.
In case of a finite horizon,
the
indifference prior

makes all actions equally preferable to AIXI (
Section 5.2.1
).
The
dogmatic prior

makes AIXI stick to any given computable policy
\pi
as long as
expected future rewards do not fall too close to zero (
Section 5.2.2
).
The
Gödel prior

prevents AIXI
tl
from taking any actions (
Section 5.2.3
).

5.2.1
The Indifference Prior

The following theorem constructs the
indifference prior

that yields a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
that
causes argmax ties for the first
m
steps.
If we use a discount function that only cares about the first
m
steps,

\Gamma_{m}=0
,
then all policies are
\xi^{\prime}
-optimal policies.
In this case AIXI’s behavior only depends on how we break argmax ties.

Theorem 5.4

(Indifference Prior)
.

If there is an
m
such that
\Gamma_{m}=0
,
then there is a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
such that
all policies are
\xi^{\prime}
-optimal.

Proof.

First, we assume that the action space is binary,
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
.
Let
U
be the reference UTM and
define the UTM
U^{\prime}
by

U^{\prime}(s_{<m}p,a_{1:t})~:=~U(p,a_{1:t}\xor s_{1:t}),

where
s_{<m}
is a binary string of length
m-1
and
s_{k}:=0
for
k\geq m
.
(
U^{\prime}
has no programs of length less than
m-1
.)
Let
\xi^{\prime}
be the Bayesian mixture given by
U^{\prime}

according to (
4.8
).
Then

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}(e_{<m}\parallel a_{<m})~

\displaystyle=~\sum_{p:\,e_{<m}\sqsubseteq U^{\prime}(p,a_{<m})}2^{-|p|}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{s_{<m}p^{\prime}:\,e_{<m}\sqsubseteq U^{\prime}(s_{<m}p^{\prime},a_{<m})}2^{-m-1-|p^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{s_{<m}}~\sum_{p^{\prime}:\,e_{<m}\sqsubseteq U(p^{\prime},a_{<m}\xor s_{<m})}2^{-m-1-|p^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{s_{<m}}~\sum_{p^{\prime}:\,e_{<m}\sqsubseteq U(p^{\prime},s_{<m})}2^{-m-1-|p^{\prime}|},

which is independent of
a_{<m}
.
Hence the first
m-1
percepts are independent of the first
m-1
actions.
But the percepts’ rewards from time step
m
on do not matter
since
\Gamma_{m}=0
(
Section 4.2.2
).
Because the environment is chronological,
the value function must be independent of all actions.
Thus every policy is
\xi^{\prime}
-optimal.

For finite action spaces
\mathcal{A}
with more than
2
elements,
the proof works analogously
by making
\mathcal{A}
a cyclic group and using the group operation instead of
\xor
.
∎

The choice of
U^{\prime}
in the proof of
Section 5.2.1

depends on
m
.
If we increase AIXI’s horizon
while fixing the UTM
U^{\prime}
,

Section 5.2.1
no longer holds.
For Solomonoff induction, there is an analogous problem:
when using Solomonoff’s prior
M
to predict a deterministic binary sequence
x
,
we make at most
K(x)
errors (
Section 3.6.1
).
In case the shortest program has length
>m
,
there is no guarantee that we make less than
m
errors
(see
Section 5.6.2
).

5.2.2
The Dogmatic Prior

In this section
we define a universal prior
that assigns very high probability of going to hell (reward
0
forever)
if we deviate from a given computable policy
\pi
.
For a Bayesian agent like AIXI,
it is thus only worth deviating from the policy
\pi

if the agent thinks that the prospects of following
\pi
are very poor already.
We call this prior the
dogmatic prior
,
because the fear of going to hell makes AIXI conform to
any arbitrary ‘dogmatic ideology’
\pi
.
AIXI will only break out
if it expects
\pi
to give very low future payoff;
in that case the agent does not have much to lose.

Theorem 5.5

(Dogmatic Prior)
.

Let
\pi
be any computable deterministic policy,
let
\xi
be any Bayesian mixture over
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
, and
let
\varepsilon>0
.
There is a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
such that
for any history
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}
consistent with
\pi
and
for which
V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})>\varepsilon
,
the action
\pi(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})
is the unique
\xi^{\prime}
-optimal action.

The following proof was adapted from
Leike and Hutter 2015c

to work for environment classes that do not contain the Bayesian mixture.
Essentially, for every environment
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}

the dogmatic prior puts much higher weight on an environment
\rho_{\nu}

that behaves just like
\nu
on the policy
\pi
,
but sends any policy deviating from
\pi
to hell.
Importantly, while following the policy
\pi

the environments
\nu
and
\rho_{\nu}
are indistinguishable,
so the posterior belief in
\nu
is equal to the posterior belief in
\rho_{\nu}
.

Proof of
Section 5.2.2
.

We assume
(o,0)\in\mathcal{E}
for some
o\in\mathcal{O}
.
For every environment
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
define the environment

\rho_{\nu}(e_{1:t}\parallel a_{1:t}):=\begin{cases}\nu(e_{1:t}\parallel a_{1:t}),&\text{if }a_{k}=\pi(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k})\;\forall k\leq t,\\
\nu(e_{<k}\parallel a_{<k}),&\text{if }k:=\min\{i\mid a_{i}\neq\pi(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<i})\}\text{ exists}\\
&\text{ and }e_{i}=(o,0)\;\forall i\in\{k,\ldots,t\},\text{ and}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The environment
\rho_{\nu}
mimics environment
\nu

until it receives an action that the policy
\pi
would not take.
From then on, it provides rewards
0
.
Since
\pi
is a computable policy,
we have that
\rho_{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
for every
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
.

Now we need to reweigh the prior
w
so that it assigns
a much higher prior weight to
\rho_{\nu}
than to
\nu
.
Without loss of generality we assume that
\varepsilon
is computable,
otherwise we make it slightly smaller.
We define
w^{\prime}(\nu):=\varepsilon w(\nu)
if
\nu\neq\rho_{\tilde{\nu}}

for all
\tilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
and

w^{\prime}(\rho_{\nu}):=(1-\varepsilon)w(\nu)+\varepsilon w(\rho_{\nu})
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}}w^{\prime}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu=\rho_{\tilde{\nu}}}w^{\prime}(\nu)+\sum_{\nu\neq\rho_{\tilde{\nu}}}w^{\prime}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu=\rho_{\tilde{\nu}}}\big((1-\varepsilon)w(\tilde{\nu})+\varepsilon w(\nu)\big)+\sum_{\nu\neq\rho_{\tilde{\nu}}}\varepsilon w(\nu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}}\varepsilon w(\nu)+\sum_{\tilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}}(1-\varepsilon)w(\tilde{\nu})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon+(1-\varepsilon)=1,

and with
w^{\prime}\geq\varepsilon w
, we get that

w^{\prime}
is a positive prior over
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
.
We define
\xi^{\prime}
as the corresponding Bayesian mixture
analogous to (
4.5
).

With
\rho:=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}}w(\nu)\rho_{\nu}

we get
\xi^{\prime}=\varepsilon\xi+(1-\varepsilon)\rho
.
The mixtures
\xi
and
\rho
coincide on the policy
\pi
since
every
\nu
coincides with
\rho_{\nu}
on the policy
\pi
:

\xi^{\pi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}}w(\nu)\nu^{\pi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}}w(\nu)\rho_{\nu}^{\pi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})=\rho^{\pi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

Moreover,
V^{*}_{\rho_{\nu}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})=0
and thus
V^{*}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})=0

for any history inconsistent with
\pi
by construction of
\rho_{\nu}
.

Let
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\in(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E})^{*}
be any history consistent with
\pi
such that

V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})>\varepsilon
.
Then
\rho^{\pi}=\xi^{\pi}
implies

\frac{\rho(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}{\xi^{\prime}(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}=\frac{\xi(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}{\xi^{\prime}(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}=\frac{\xi(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}{\varepsilon\xi(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})+(1-\varepsilon)\rho(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}=1.

Therefore
Section 4.2.2
implies that for all
a\in\mathcal{A}

and all policies
\tilde{\pi}

\displaystyle V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}a)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\frac{\xi(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}{\xi^{\prime}(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}a)+(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\rho(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}{\xi^{\prime}(e_{<t}\parallel a_{<t})}V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}a)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}a)+(1-\varepsilon)V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}a).

(5.1)

Let
\alpha:=\pi(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})
be the next action according to
\pi
, and
let
\beta\neq\alpha
be any other action.
We have that
V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\alpha)=V^{\pi}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\alpha)

since
\xi^{\pi}=\rho^{\pi}
and
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\alpha
is consistent with
\pi
.
Therefore we get from (
5.1
)

\displaystyle V^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\alpha)

\displaystyle\geq V^{\pi}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\alpha)=\varepsilon V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\alpha)+(1-\varepsilon)V^{\pi}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\alpha)=V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\alpha)>\varepsilon,

\displaystyle V^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\beta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\beta)+(1-\varepsilon)V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\beta)=\varepsilon V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}a)+(1-\varepsilon)0\leq\varepsilon.

Hence
V^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\alpha)>V^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\beta)

and thus the action
\alpha
taken by
\pi
is
the only
\xi^{\prime}
-optimal action for the history
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}
.
∎

Corollary 5.6

(With Finite Horizon Every Policy is Bayes Optimal)
.

If
\Gamma_{m}=0
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
then for any deterministic policy
\pi

there is a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
such that

\pi(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})
is the only
\xi^{\prime}
-optimal action for all histories
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}

consistent with
\pi
and
t\leq m
.

In contrast to
Section 5.2.1

where every policy is
\xi^{\prime}
-optimal for a fixed Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
,

Section 5.2.2
gives
a different Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}

for every policy
\pi
such that

\pi
is the
only

\xi^{\prime}
-optimal policy.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be small enough such that

V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})>\varepsilon
for all
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}
and
t\leq m
.
(This is possible because
(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E})^{m}
is finite by

Section 4.1.2
c
.)
We use the dogmatic prior from
Section 5.2.2

to construct a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}

for the policy
\pi
and
\varepsilon>0
.
Thus for any history
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}\in(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E})^{*}
consistent with
\pi
and
t\leq m
,
the action
\pi(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})
is the only
\xi^{\prime}
-optimal action.
∎

Corollary 5.7

(AIXI Emulating Computable Policies)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
let
\pi
be any computable policy.
There is a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
such that
for any
\xi^{\prime}
-optimal policy
\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}
and
for any environment
\nu
,

\left|V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}}_{\nu}(\epsilon)-V^{\pi}_{\nu}(\epsilon)\right|~<~\varepsilon.

Proof.

From the proof of
Section 5.2.2

and
Section 4.2.2
.
∎

5.2.3
The Gödel Prior

This section introduces a prior that
prevents any fixed formal system from making any
statements about the outcome of all but finitely many computations.
It is named after
Gödel 1931

who famously showed that for any sufficiently rich formal system
there are statements that it can neither prove nor disprove.

This prior is targeted at AIXI
tl
,
a computable approximation to AIXI
defined by
Hutter 2005
.
AIXI
tl
aims to perform as least as well as the best agent
who is limited by time
t
and space
l

that can be verified using a proof of length at most
n

for some fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The core idea is to enumerate all deterministic policies and proofs and
then execute the policy for which the best value has been proved.

In order to be verified,
a policy
\pi
has to be computed by a program
p
which fulfills
the
verification condition

\mathrm{VA}(p)

(
Hutter 2005
, Eq. 7.7)
.
This program
p
not only computes future actions of
\pi
,
but also hypothetical past actions
a^{\prime}_{i}
and
lower bounds
v_{i}
for the value of the policy
\pi
:

\mathrm{VA}(p)~:=~\text{``}\forall k\forall(va^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}})_{1:k}.\;\big(p(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k})=v_{1}a^{\prime}_{1}\ldots v_{k}a^{\prime}_{k}\rightarrow v_{k}\leq V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k})\big)\text{''},

where
\pi
is the policy derived from
p
according to

\pi(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}):=a^{\prime}_{k}
.

We fix some formal system
that we use to prove the verification condition.
We want it to be sufficiently powerful,
but this incurs Gödel incompleteness.
For simplicity of exposition we pick
\mathsf{PA}
,
the system of
Peano arithmetic

(
Shoenfield 1967
, Ch. 8.1)
,
but our result generalizes trivially to all formal systems
which cannot prove their own consistency.

Let
n
be a fixed constant.
The algorithm for AIXI
tl
is specified as follows.

1.

Let
P=\emptyset
.
This will be the set of verified programs.

2.

For all proofs in
\mathsf{PA}
of length
\leq n
:
if the proof proves
\mathrm{VA}(p)
for some
p
,
and
|p|\leq l
,
then add the program
p
to
P
.

3.

For each input history
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k}
repeat:
run all programs from
P
for at most
t
steps each,
take the one with the highest promised value
v_{k}
,
and return that program’s policy’s action.

Theorem 5.8

(The Gödel Prior)
.

There is a UTM
U^{\prime}
such that
if
\mathsf{PA}
is consistent,
then the set of verified programs
P
is empty for all
t
,
l
, and
n
.

Proof.

Let
q
denote an algorithm that never halts,
but for which this cannot be proved in
\mathsf{PA}
;
e.g., let
q
enumerate all consequences of
\mathsf{PA}

and halt as soon as it finds a contradiction.
Since we assumed that
\mathsf{PA}
is consistent,
q
never halts.
Define the UTM
U^{\prime}(p,a_{1:k})
as follows.

•

Run
q
for
k
steps.

•

If
q
halts, output
v_{k}=2
.

•

Run
U(p,a_{1:k})
.

Since
q
never halts,
U
and
U^{\prime}
are functionally identical,
therefore
U^{\prime}
is universal.
Note that
\mathsf{PA}
proves

\forall p.\;U(p,a_{1:k})=U^{\prime}(p,a_{1:k})
for any fixed
k
,
but
\mathsf{PA}
does not prove

\forall k\forall p.\;U(p,a_{1:k})=U^{\prime}(p,a_{1:k})
.

If
q
did eventually halt,
it would output a value
v_{k}=2
that is too high,
since the value function
V^{\pi}_{\xi}
is bounded by
1
from above,
which
\mathsf{PA}
knows.
Hence
\mathsf{PA}
proves that

q\text{ halts}\rightarrow\forall p.\;\neg\mathrm{VA}(p)

(5.2)

If
\mathsf{PA}
could prove
\mathrm{VA}(p)
for any
p
,
then
\mathsf{PA}
would prove that
q
does not halt
since this is the contrapositive of (
5.2
).
Therefore the set
P
remains empty.
∎

AIXI
tl
exhibits all the problems of the arbitrariness of the UTM
illustrated by the indifference prior (
Section 5.2.1
) and
the dogmatic prior (
Section 5.2.2
).
In addition, it is also susceptible to Gödel incompleteness
as illustrated by the Gödel prior in
Section 5.2.3
.
The formal system that is a parameter to AIXI
tl

just provides another point of failure.

As a computable approximation to AIXI,
AIXI
tl
is needlessly complicated.
As we prove in
Section 6.3.1
,

\varepsilon
-optimal AIXI is limit computable,
so we can approximate it with an anytime algorithm.
Bounding the computational resources to the approximation algorithm
already yields a computable version of AIXI.
Moreover, unlike AIXI
tl
, this approximation
actually converges to AIXI in the limit.
Furthermore, we can ‘speed up’ this approximation algorithm
using
Hutter search

(
Hutter 2002b
)
;
this is very similar but not identical to AIXI
tl
.

5.3
Bayes Optimality

The aim of the Legg-Hutter intelligence measure is to formalize the intuitive
notion of intelligence mathematically.

Legg and Hutter 2007a
collect various definitions of intelligence
across many academic fields and destill it into the following statement
(
Legg and Hutter 2007b
)

Intelligence measures an agent’s ability to achieve goals in a wide range of
environments.

This definition is formalized as follows.

Definition 5.9

(Legg-Hutter Intelligence;
Legg and Hutter 2007b
, Sec. 3.3
))
.

The
(Legg-Hutter) intelligence
of a policy
\pi
is defined as

\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi):=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu)V^{\pi}_{\nu}(\epsilon)

The Legg-Hutter intelligence of a policy
\pi
is
the
t_{0}
-value that
\pi
achieves across all environments from the class
\mathcal{M}

weighted by the prior
w
.

Legg and Hutter 2007b
consider a subclass of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
,
the class of computable measures
together with a Solomonoff prior
w(\nu)=2^{-K(\nu)}

and do not use discounting explicitly.

Typically, the index
\xi
is omitted when writing
\Upsilon
.
However, in this section we consider
the intelligence measure with respect to different priors,
therefore we make this dependency explicit.
The following proposition motivates the use of the index
\xi
instead of
w
.

Proposition 5.10

(Bayes Optimality = Maximal Intelligence)
.

\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)=V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\epsilon)
for all policies
\pi
.

Proof.

Follows directly from (
4.6
) and
Section 5.3
.
∎

Definition 5.11

(Balanced Pareto Optimality;
Hutter 2005
, Def. 5.22
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a set of environments.
A policy
\pi
is

balanced Pareto optimal in the set of environments
\mathcal{M}
iff
for all policies
\tilde{\pi}
,

\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu)\left(V^{\pi}_{\nu}(\epsilon)-V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\nu}(\epsilon)\right)\geq 0.

Proposition 5.12

(Balanced Pareto Optimality = Maximal Intelligence)
.

A policy
\pi
is balanced Pareto optimal in
\mathcal{M}

if and only if

\pi
has maximal Legg-Hutter intelligence.

Proof.

From (
4.6
) we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu)\left(V^{\pi}_{\nu}(\epsilon)-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\nu}(\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu)V^{\pi}_{\nu}(\epsilon)-\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu)V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\nu}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\epsilon)-V^{*}_{\xi}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\epsilon)-\sup_{\tilde{\pi}}V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\xi}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)-\sup_{\tilde{\pi}}\Upsilon(\tilde{\pi})

by
Section 5.3
.
This term is nonnegative iff
\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)
is maximal.
∎

As a consequence of
Section 5.3
and

Section 5.3
we get that
AIXI is balanced Pareto optimal
(
Hutter 2005
, Thm. 5.24)

and has maximal Legg-Hutter intelligence.

\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}~:=~\sup_{\pi}\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)~=~\sup_{\pi}V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\epsilon)~=~V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\xi}(\epsilon)~=~\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi^{*}_{\xi}).

This is not surprising since Legg-Hutter intelligence was defined
in terms of the
t_{0}
-value in the Bayes mixture.
Moreover, because the value function is scaled to be in the interval
[0,1]
,
intelligence is a real number between
0
and
1
.

It is just as hard to score very high on the Legg-Hutter intelligence measure
as it is to score very low:
we can always turn a reward minimizer into a reward maximizer by inverting
the rewards
r_{t}^{\prime}:=1-r_{t}
.
Hence the lowest possible intelligence score is achieved by
AIXI’s twin sister, a
\xi
-expected reward minimizer:

\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}~:=~\inf_{\pi}\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)~=~\inf_{\pi}V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\epsilon)

The heaven environment (reward
1
forever) and
the hell environment (reward
0
forever) are computable
and thus in the environment class
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
;
therefore it is impossible
to get a reward
0
or reward
1
in every environment.
Consequently, for all policies
\pi
,

0~<~\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}~\leq~\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)~\leq~\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}~<~1.

For every real number
r\in[\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi},\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}]

there is a policy
\pi
with
\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)=r
:
analogously to
Section 4.2.2
we can define
\pi
such that
with probability
(r-\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi})/(\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}-\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi})
it follows
\pi^{*}_{\xi}
and
otherwise it follows
\argmin_{\tilde{\pi}}V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\xi}(\epsilon)
.

Figure 5.1
illustrates the intelligence measure
\Upsilon
.
It is natural to fix the policy
random

that takes actions uniformly at random
to have an intelligence score of
1/2

by choosing a ‘symmetric’ universal prior
(
Legg and Veness 2013
)
.

0
1
\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}
\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}
random
AI
\xi^{\prime}
AI
\xi
image of
\Upsilon

Figure 5.1
:
The Legg-Hutter intelligence measure assigns values
within the closed interval
[\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi},\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}]
;
the assigned values are depicted in purple.
By
Section 5.3
,
computable policies are dense in this purple set.

AIXI is not computable (
Section 6.3.2
),
hence there is no computable policy
\pi
such that

\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)=\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}
or

\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)=\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}
for any Bayesian mixture
\xi

over
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
.
But the following theorem states that
computable policies can come arbitrarily close.
This is no surprise:
by
Section 4.2.2

we can do well on a Legg-Hutter intelligence test
simply by memorizing what AIXI would do for the first
k
steps;
as long as
k
is chosen large enough such that
discounting makes the remaining rewards contribute
very little to the value function.

Theorem 5.13

(Computable Policies are Dense)
.

The set

\{\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)\mid\pi\text{ is a computable policy}\}

is dense in the set
[\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi},\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}]
.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be any policy and let
\varepsilon>0
.
We need to show that there is a computable policy
\tilde{\pi}
with

|\Upsilon_{\xi}(\tilde{\pi})-\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)|<\varepsilon
.
We choose
m
large enough such that
\Gamma_{m}/\Gamma_{1}<\varepsilon/3
.
Let
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
be arbitrary and define the policy

\tilde{\pi}(a\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})~:=~\begin{cases}\pi(a\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\pm(\varepsilon/3)^{-m}&\text{if }t<m,\\
1&\text{if $t\geq m$ and $a=\alpha$},\text{ and}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

By choosing an appropriate rational number in the interval

[\pi(a\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-(\varepsilon/3)^{-m},\pi(a\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})+(\varepsilon/3)^{-m}]

we can make the policy
\tilde{\pi}
computable because we can store
these approximations to the action probabilities of
\pi

for the first
m-1
steps in a lookup table.
From
Section 4.2.2
we get

\left|V^{\pi,m}_{\xi}(\epsilon)-V^{\tilde{\pi},m}_{\xi}(\epsilon)\right|\leq D_{m-1}(\xi^{\pi},\xi^{\tilde{\pi}}\mid\epsilon)\leq\left((\varepsilon/3)^{-m}\right)^{m}=\frac{\varepsilon}{3}

and together with
Section 4.2.2
this yields

\left|\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)-\Upsilon_{\xi}(\tilde{\pi})\right|=\left|V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\epsilon)-V^{\tilde{\pi}}_{\xi}(\epsilon)\right|\leq\left|V^{\pi,m}_{\xi}(\epsilon)-V^{\tilde{\pi},m}_{\xi}(\epsilon)\right|+2\frac{\Gamma_{m}}{\Gamma_{1}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+2\frac{\Gamma_{m}}{\Gamma_{1}}<\varepsilon.\qed

Remark 5.14

(Deterministic Policies are not Dense in

[\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi},\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}]
)
.

The intelligence values of deterministic policies are generally not
dense in the interval
[\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi},\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}]
.
We show this by defining an environment
\nu
where
the first action determines whether
the agent goes to heaven or hell:
action
\alpha
leads to heaven and action
\beta
leads to hell.
Define Bayesian mixture

\xi^{\prime}:=0.999\nu+0.001\xi
and
let
\pi
be any policy.
If
\pi
takes action
\alpha
first,
then
\Upsilon_{\xi^{\prime}}(\pi)>0.999
.
If
\pi
takes action
\beta
first,
then
\Upsilon_{\xi^{\prime}}(\pi)<0.001
.
Hence there are no deterministic policies that
score an intelligence value in the closed interval
[0.001,0.999]
.
∎

Legg-Hutter intelligence is measured
with respect to a fixed prior.
The Bayes agent is the most intelligent policy

if it uses the same prior
.
We use the results from
Section 5.2

to show that the intelligence score of the Bayes agent
can be arbitrary close to
the minimum intelligence score
\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}
.

Corollary 5.15

(Some AIXIs are Stupid)
.

For any Bayesian mixture
\xi
over
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
and every
\varepsilon>0
,
there is a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
such that

\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}})<\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}+\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
.
According to
Section 5.3
,
there is a computable policy
\pi
such that

\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)<\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}+\varepsilon/2
.
From
Section 5.2.2
we get a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
such that

|\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}})-\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)|=|V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}}_{\xi}(\epsilon)-V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\epsilon)|<\varepsilon/2
,
hence

|\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}})-\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}|\leq|\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}})-\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)|+|\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi)-\underline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}|<\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/2=\varepsilon.\qed

We get the same result if we fix AIXI, but rig the intelligence measure.

Corollary 5.16

(AIXI is Stupid for Some
\Upsilon
)
.

For any deterministic
\xi
-optimal policy
\pi^{*}_{\xi}
and
for every
\varepsilon>0

there is a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
such that

\Upsilon_{\xi^{\prime}}(\pi^{*}_{\xi})\leq\varepsilon
and

\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi^{\prime}}\geq 1-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Let
a_{1}:=\pi^{*}_{\xi}(\epsilon)
be the first action that
\pi^{*}_{\xi}
takes.
We define an environment
\nu
such that
taking the first action
a_{1}
leads to hell and
taking any other first action leads to heaven
as in
Section 5.3
.
We define the Bayesian mixture

\xi^{\prime}:=(1-\varepsilon)\nu+\varepsilon\xi
.
Since
\pi^{*}_{\xi}
takes action
a_{1}
first,
it goes to hell, i.e.,
V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\nu}(\epsilon)=0
.
Hence with
Section 4.2.2

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\xi^{\prime}}(\pi^{*}_{\xi})~

\displaystyle=~V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\epsilon)~=~(1-\varepsilon)V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\nu}(\epsilon)+\varepsilon V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\xi}(\epsilon)~\leq~\varepsilon.

For any policy
\pi
that
takes an action other than
a_{1}
first,
we get

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\xi^{\prime}}(\pi)~

\displaystyle=~V^{\pi}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\epsilon)~=~(1-\varepsilon)V^{\pi}_{\nu}(\epsilon)+\varepsilon V^{\pi}_{\xi}(\epsilon)~\geq~1-\varepsilon.\qed

On the other hand,
we can make any computable policy smart
if we choose the right Bayesian mixture.
In particular, we get that there is a Bayesian mixture such that
‘do nothing’ is the most intelligent policy save for some
\varepsilon
.

Corollary 5.17

(Computable Policies can be Smart)
.

For any computable policy
\pi
and any
\varepsilon>0

there is a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
such that

\Upsilon_{\xi^{\prime}}(\pi)>\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi^{\prime}}-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Section 5.2.2
yields a Bayesian mixture
\xi^{\prime}
with

|\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi^{\prime}}-\Upsilon_{\xi^{\prime}}(\pi)|=|V^{*}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\epsilon)-V^{\pi}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\epsilon)|<\varepsilon
.
∎

5.4
Asymptotic Optimality

An asymptotically optimal policy is a policy
learns to act optimally in every environment from
\mathcal{M}
,
i.e., the value of this policy converges to the optimal value.

Definition 5.18

(Asymptotic Optimality)
.

A policy
\pi
is

asymptotically optimal in an environment class
\mathcal{M}

iff for all
\mu\in\mathcal{M}

V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\to 0\text{ as $t\to\infty$}

(5.3)

on histories drawn from
\mu^{\pi}
.

name

definition

strong a.o.

V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\to 0

\mu^{\pi}
-almost surely

a.o. in mean

{\mathbb{E}}^{\pi}_{\mu}\left[V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right]\to 0

a.o. in probability

\forall\varepsilon>0.\;\mu^{\pi}\left[V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})>\varepsilon\right]\to 0

weak a.o.

\frac{1}{t}\sum_{k=1}^{t}\Big(V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k})-V^{\pi}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k})\Big)\to 0

\mu^{\pi}
-almost surely

Table 5.1
:

The formal definition of different types of asymptotic optimality.
In each case we understand the limit as
t\to\infty
.

There are different types of asymptotic optimality
based on the type of stochastic convergence in (
5.3
);
see
Section 2.2
.
If this convergence occurs almost surely,
it is called
strong asymptotic optimality

(
Lattimore and Hutter 2011
, Def. 7)
;
if this convergence occurs in mean,
it is called
asymptotic optimality in mean
;
if this convergence occurs in probability,
it is called
asymptotic optimality in probability
;
and
if the Cesàro averages converge almost surely,
it is called
weak asymptotic optimality

(
Lattimore and Hutter 2011
, Def. 7)
.
Since the value function is a nonnegative bounded random variable,
asymptotic optimality in mean and asymptotic optimality in probability
are equivalent.
See
Table 5.1
for the explicit definitions and
see
Figure 5.2
for an overview over
their relationship.

strong a.o.
weak a.o.
a.o. in mean
a.o. in probability

Figure 5.2
:

The relationship between different types of asymptotic optimality.
Each arrow indicates a logical implication and
each lack of an arrow indicates that there is no logical implication.

Asymptotic optimality in probability is in spirit
a probably approximately correct (PAC) result:
for all
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta>0

the probability that our policy is
\varepsilon
-suboptimal converges to zero;
eventually this probability will be less than
\delta
.
For a PAC result it is typically demanded that the number of time steps
until the probability is less than
\delta

be polynomial in
1/\varepsilon
and
1/\delta
.
In general environments this is impossible,
and here we have no ambition to provide concrete convergence rates.

Intuitively, a necessary condition for asymptotic optimality is
that the agent needs to explore infinitely often for an entire effective horizon.
If we explore only finitely often,
then the environment might change after we stopped exploring.
Moreover, the agent needs to predict
the value of counterfactual policies accurately;
but by
Section 4.2.2
only for an
\varepsilon
-effective horizon.
By committing to exploration for the entire effective horizon,
we learn about the value of counterfactual policies.

Example 5.19

(Exploration Infinitely Often for an Entire Effective Horizon)
.

If there is an
\varepsilon>0
such that
the policy
\pi
does not explore for
H_{t}(\varepsilon)
steps infinitely often,
then
V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})>\varepsilon
infinitely often.
Define
\mathcal{A}:=\{\alpha,\beta\}
and
\mathcal{E}:=\{0,\varepsilon/2,1\}
(observations are vacuous) and
consider the following class of environments

\mathcal{M}:=\{\nu_{\infty},\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots\}

(transitions are labeled with condition: action, reward):

s_{0}
\beta,\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
\alpha,0

s_{0}
s_{1}
\ldots
s_{n}
\beta,\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
t<k:\alpha,0
t\geq k:\alpha,0
\alpha,0
\alpha,0
\beta,0
\beta,0
\beta,0
\alpha,1

\nu_{\infty}

\nu_{k}

Environment
\nu_{k}
works just like environment
\nu_{\infty}
,
except that at time step
k
the path to state
s_{1}
gets unlocked.
The length of the state sequence in
\nu_{k}
is defined as
an
\varepsilon
-effective horizon,
n:=H_{t}(\varepsilon)

where
t
is the time step in which the agent leaves state
s_{0}
.
The optimal policy in environment
\nu_{\infty}
is to always take action
\beta
,
the optimal policy for environment
\nu_{k}
is
to take action
\beta
for
t<k
and then take action
\alpha
.
Suppose the agent is in time step
t
and in state
s_{0}
.
Since these environments are partially observable,
it needs to explore for
n
steps (take action
\alpha

n
times)
to distinguish
\nu_{\infty}
from
\nu_{k}
for any
k\leq t
.
Since there are infinitely many
\nu_{k}
,
the agent needs to do this infinitely often.
Moreover,
V^{*}_{\nu_{1}}\geq\varepsilon
and
V^{*}_{\nu_{\infty}}=\varepsilon/2
,
so if
\nu_{t}
is the true environment,
then not exploring to the right for an
\varepsilon
-effective horizon
is suboptimal by
\varepsilon/2
.
But if
\nu_{\infty}
is the true environment,
then exploring incurs an opportunity cost of one reward of
\varepsilon/2
.
∎

Next, we state two negative results about asymptotic optimality
proved by
Lattimore and Hutter 2011
.
It is important to emphasize that

Section 5.4
and
Section 5.4

only hold for deterministic policies.

Theorem 5.20

(Deterministic Policies are not Strongly Asymptotically Optimal;

Lattimore and Hutter 2011
, Thm. 8
)
.

There is no deterministic policy
that is strong asymptotically optimal in the class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{\mathrm{CCM}}
.

If the horizon grows linearly
(for example, power discounting
\gamma(t)=t^{-\beta}

with
\beta>1
; see
Table 4.1
),
then a deterministic policy cannot be weakly asymptotically optimal policy:
the agent has to explore for an entire effective horizon,
which prevents the Cesàro average from converging.

Theorem 5.21

(Necessary Condition for Weak Asymptotic Optimality;

Lattimore 2013
, Thm. 5.5
)
.

If there is an
\varepsilon>0
such that
H_{t}(\varepsilon)\notin o(t)
,
then there is no deterministic policy
that is weakly asymptotically optimal in the class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{\mathrm{CCM}}
.

There are several agents that achieve asymptotic optimality.
In the rest of this section, we discuss the Bayes agent,

BayesExp
, and Thompson sampling.
Asymptotic optimality can also be achieved
through optimism
(
Sunehag and Hutter 2012a
;
Sunehag and Hutter 2012b
;
Sunehag and Hutter 2015
)
.

5.4.1
Bayes

In this section, we list two results from the literature
regarding the asymptotic optimality of the Bayes optimal policy.
The following negative result is due to
Orseau 2010
;
Orseau 2013
.

Theorem 5.22

(Bayes is not Asymptotically Optimal in General Environments;

Orseau 2013
, Thm. 4
)
.

For any class
\mathcal{M}\supseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{\mathrm{CCM}}

no Bayes optimal policy
\pi^{*}_{\xi}
is asymptotically optimal:
there is an environment
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
and
a time step
t_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mu^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}
-almost surely for all time steps
t\geq t_{0}

V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})=\frac{1}{2}.

Orseau calls this result the
good enough effect
:
A Bayesian agent eventually decides that the current strategy is good enough
and that any additional exploration is not worth its expected payoff.
However, if the environment changes afterwards,
the Bayes agent is acting suboptimally.

Proof.

Without loss of generality
assume
\mathcal{A}:=\{\alpha,\beta\}
and
\mathcal{E}:=\{0,1/2,1\}
(observations are vacuous).
We consider the following environment
\mu
(transitions are labeled with action, reward).
s_{0}
s_{1}
\ldots
s_{n}
\beta,\frac{1}{2}
\alpha,0
\ast,0
\ast,0
\ast,0

In state
s_{0}
the action
\beta
is the exploitation action and
the action
\alpha
the exploration action.
The length of the state sequence is defined as
an
1/t
-effective horizon,
n:=H_{t}(1/t)

where
t
is the time step in which the agent leaves state
s_{0}
.
Since the discount function
\gamma
is computable
by
Section 4.1.2
a
,

\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
.

Assume that when acting in
\mu
,
the Bayes agent explores infinitely often.
Let
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}
be a history in which
the agent is in state
s_{0}
and takes action
\alpha
.
Then
V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\mu}\leq 1/t
.
By on-policy value convergence (
Section 4.2.3
),

V^{*}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\to 0

\mu^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}
-almost surely.
Hence there is a time step
t_{0}
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}

we have
V^{*}_{\xi}<w(\mu)/2
.
Since
\mu
is deterministic,
w(\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\geq w(\mu)
.
Now we get a contradiction from (
4.7
):

V^{*}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\geq w(\mu|\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\geq w(\mu)V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})=\frac{w(\mu)}{2}>V^{*}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

Therefore the Bayes agent stops taking the exploration action
\alpha

after time step
t_{0}
,
and so it is not optimal in any
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
that behaves like
\mu

until time step
t_{0}
and then changes:
s_{0}
s_{1}
\ldots
s_{n}
\beta,\frac{1}{2}
t>t_{0}:\alpha,1
t\leq t_{0}:\alpha,0
\ast,0
\ast,0
\ast,0

∎

The following theorem is also known as
the
self-optimizing theorem
.
This theorem has been a source of great confusion because
its statement in
Hutter 2005
is not very explicit
about how the histories are generated.
The formulation of
Lattimore 2013

is explicit, but less general.

Theorem 5.23

(Sufficient Condition for Strong Asymptotic Optimality of Bayes;
Hutter 2005
, Thm. 5.34
)
.

Let
\mu
be some environment.
If there is a policy
\pi
and
a sequence of policies
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\ldots
such that
for all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}

V^{*}_{\nu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi_{t}}_{\nu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\to 0\text{ as $t\to\infty$ $\mu^{\pi}$-almost surely,}

(5.4)

then

V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\to 0\text{ as $t\to\infty$ $\mu^{\pi}$-almost surely.}

If
\pi=\pi^{*}_{\xi}
and
(
5.4
) holds for all
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
,
then
\pi^{*}_{\xi}
is strongly asymptotically optimal in the class
\mathcal{M}
.

It is important to emphasize that
the policies
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\ldots
need to converge to the optimal value
on the history generated by
\mu
and
\pi
,
and not (as one might think)
\nu
and
\pi_{t}
.
Intuitively, the policy
\pi
is an ‘exploration policy’
that ensures that the environment class is explored sufficiently.
Typically, a policy is asymptotically optimal on its own history.
So if
\pi=\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=\ldots
,
then we get that Bayes is asymptotically optimal on the history generated
by the policy
\pi
, not its own history.
In light of
Section 5.2.2
and
Section 5.4.1

this is not too surprising;
Bayesian reinforcement learning agents might not explore enough to be
asymptotically optimal, but given a policy that does explore enough,
Bayes learns enough to be asymptotically optimal.

This invites us to define the following policies
\pi_{t}
:
follow the information-seeking policy
\pi^{*}_{{\mathrm{IG}}}
until time step
t
,
and then follow
\pi^{*}_{\xi}
(explore until
t
, then exploit).
Since the information-seeking policy explores enough to prove
off-policy prediction
(
Orseau et al. 2013
, Thm. 7)
,
we get
V^{\pi}_{\xi}-V^{\pi}_{\mu}\to 0
for every policy
\pi
uniformly.
Hence
\argmax_{\pi}V^{\pi}_{\xi}\to\argmax_{\pi}V^{\pi}_{\mu}

and thus
V^{*}_{\mu}-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\mu}\to 0
and
(
5.4
) is satisfied.
From
Section 5.4.1
we get
V^{*}_{\mu}-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\mu}\to 0
,
which we already knew.
In order to get strong asymptotic optimality,
all we need to do is choose the switching time step
t
appropriately,
i.e., wait until
V^{*}_{\mu}
and
V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\mu}
are close enough.
Unfortunately, this is an invalid strategy:
the agent does not know the true environment
\mu

and hence cannot check this condition.

Hutter 2005
uses
Section 5.4.1
to show that
the Bayes optimal policy is strongly asymptotically optimal
in the class of ergodic finite-state MDPs if the effective horizon is growing,
i.e.,
H_{t}(\varepsilon)\to\infty
for all
\varepsilon>0
.
This relies on the fact that in ergodic finite-state MDPs
we need a fixed number of steps to explore the entire environment
up to
\varepsilon
-confidence.
Therefore we can define a sequence of policies
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\ldots

that completely disregard the history and start exploring everything from scratch.
Since the effective horizon is growing,
this exploration phase takes a vanishing fraction of effective horizon
and most of the value is retained.
Therefore the sequence of policies
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\ldots

satisfies the condition of
Section 5.4.1
regardless of the history,
thus in particular for the history generated by

\pi=\pi^{*}_{\xi}
and any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
.
Note that the condition on the horizon is important:
If the effective horizon is bounded,
then Bayes is not asymptotically optimal
in the class of ergodic finite-state MDPs
because it can be locked into a dogmatic prior
similarly to
Section 5.2.2
.

Proof of
Section 5.4.1
.

From (
4.6
) we get for any history
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}

\displaystyle w(\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\left(V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\left(V^{*}_{\nu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\nu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})V^{*}_{\nu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right)-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})V^{*}_{\nu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi_{t}}_{\xi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\big(V^{*}_{\nu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi_{t}}_{\nu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\big).

(5.5)

From (
5.4
) follows that

V^{*}_{\nu}-V^{\pi_{t}}_{\nu}\to 0

\mu^{\pi}
-almost surely for all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}
,
so (
5.5
)
converges to
0

\mu^{\pi}
-almost surely
(
Hutter 2005
, Lem. 5.28ii)
. Similar to
Section 3.3
,

1/w(\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})
is a nonnegative
\mu^{\pi}
-martingale and thus
converges (to a finite value)
\mu^{\pi}
-almost surely
by
Section 2.2
.
Therefore
V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi^{*}_{\xi}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\to 0

\mu^{\pi}
-almost surely.
If this is true for all
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
,
the strong asymptotic optimality of
\pi^{*}_{\xi}
follows from
\pi=\pi^{*}_{\xi}

by definition.
∎

5.4.2
BayesExp

The definition of
BayesExp
is given in
Section 4.3.3
.
In this subsection we state a result by
Lattimore 2013

that motivated the definition of
BayesExp
.

Theorem 5.24

(
BayesExp
is Weakly Asymptotically Optimal;

Lattimore 2013
, Thm. 5.6
)
.

Let
\pi_{BE}
denote the policy from
Algorithm 1
.
If
H_{t}(\varepsilon)
grows monotone in
t
and
H_{t}(\varepsilon_{t})/\varepsilon_{t}\in o(t)
,
then for all environments
\mu\in\mathcal{M}

\frac{1}{t}\sum_{k=1}^{t}\big(V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k})-V^{\pi_{BE}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k})\big)\to 0\text{ as $t\to\infty$ $\mu^{\pi_{BE}}$-almost surely}.

If the horizon grows sublinearly (
H_{t}(\varepsilon)\in o(t)
for all
\varepsilon>0
),
then we can always find a sequence
\varepsilon_{t}\to 0

that decreases slowly enough such that
H_{t}(\varepsilon)/\varepsilon_{t}\in o(t)
holds.

5.4.3
Thompson Sampling

In this section we prove that
the Thompson sampling policy defined in
Section 4.3.4

is asymptotically optimal.

Ortega and Braun 2010
prove that the action probabilities of Thompson sampling
converge to the action probability of the optimal policy almost surely,
but require a finite environment class and
two (arguably quite strong)
technical assumptions on the behavior of the posterior distribution
(akin to ergodicity)
and the similarity of environments in the class.
Our convergence results do not require these assumptions.

Theorem 5.25

(Thompson Sampling is Asymptotically Optimal in Mean)
.

For all environments
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}^{\pi_{T}}\big[V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi_{T}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\big]\to 0\text{ as $t\to\infty$.}

This theorem immediately implies that
Thompson sampling is also asymptotically optimal in probability
according to
Figure 5.2
.
However, this does not imply almost sure convergence
(see
Section 5.4.3
).

We first give an intuition for the asymptotic optimality of Thompson sampling.
At every resampling step we can split the class
\mathcal{M}
into three partitions:

1.

Environments
\rho
where
V^{\pi^{*}_{\rho}}_{\mu}\approx V^{*}_{\mu}

2.

Environments
\rho
where
V^{*}_{\rho}>V^{*}_{\mu}

3.

Environments
\rho
where
V^{*}_{\rho}<V^{*}_{\mu}

The first class is the class of ‘good’ environments:
if we draw one of them, we follow a policy that is close to optimal in
\mu
.
The second class is the class of environments that
overestimate the value of
\mu
.
Following their optimal policy the agent gains information because
rewards will be lower than expected.
The third class is the class of environments that
underestimate the value of
\mu
.
Following their optimal policy the agent might not gain information
since
\mu
might behave just like environment
\rho

on the
\rho
-optimal policy.
However, when sampling from the first class instead,
the agent gains information about the third class because rewards tend to be
better than environments from the third class predicted.

Since the true environment
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
, the first class is not empty,
and the probability of drawing a sample from the first class
does not become too small.
Whenever the second and third class
have sufficiently high weight in the posterior,
there is a good chance of picking a policy that leads the agent
to gain information.
Asymptotically, the posterior converges, so the agent ends up
having learned everything it could,
i.e., the posterior weight of the second and third class vanishes.

This argument is not too hard to formalize for deterministic environment classes.
However, for stochastic environment classes
the effect on the posterior when following a bad policy is harder to quantify
because there is always a chance that the rewards are different
simply because of bad luck.
In order to prove this theorem in its generality for stochastic classes,
we employ an entirely different proof strategy
that relies on statistical tools rather than the argument given above.

Definition 5.26

(Expected Total Variation Distance)
.

Let
\pi
be any policy and let
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\infty
.
The
expected total variation distance
on the policy
\pi
is

F^{\pi}_{m}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}):=\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{M}}w(\rho\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})D_{m}(\rho^{\pi},\xi^{\pi}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}).

If we replace the distance measure
D_{m}
by cross-entropy, then
the quantity
F^{\pi}_{m}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})
becomes the
expected information gain (see
Section 4.3.2
).

For the proof of
Section 5.4.3

we need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.27

(Expected Total Variation Distance Vanishes On-Policy)
.

For any policy
\pi
and any environment
\mu
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}^{\pi}[F^{\pi}_{\infty}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})]\to 0\text{ as $t\to\infty$.}

Proof.

From
Section 3.4.1
we get

D_{\infty}(\mu^{\pi},\xi^{\pi}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\to 0

\mu^{\pi}
-almost surely,
and since
D
is bounded, this convergence also occurs in mean.
Thus for every environment
\nu\in\mathcal{M}
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{\nu}^{\pi}\big[D_{\infty}(\nu^{\pi},\xi^{\pi}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\big]\to 0\text{ as $t\to\infty$}.

Now

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}^{\pi}[F^{\pi}_{\infty}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{w(\mu)}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi}^{\pi}[F^{\pi}_{\infty}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w(\mu)}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi}^{\pi}\left[\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})D_{\infty}(\nu^{\pi},\xi^{\pi}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w(\mu)}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi}^{\pi}\left[\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu)\frac{\nu^{\pi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})}{\xi^{\pi}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})}D_{\infty}(\nu^{\pi},\xi^{\pi}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w(\mu)}\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu){\mathbb{E}}_{\nu}^{\pi}\big[D_{\infty}(\nu^{\pi},\xi^{\pi}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\big]\to 0

by
Hutter 2005
since total variation distance is bounded.
∎

Proof of
Section 5.4.3
.

Let
\beta,\delta>0
and
let
\varepsilon_{t}>0
denote the sequence
used to define
\pi_{T}
in
Algorithm 2
.
We assume that
t
is large enough such that

\varepsilon_{k}\leq\beta
for all
k\geq t
and that

\delta
is small enough such that
w(\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})>4\delta
for all
t
,
which holds since
w(\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\not\to 0

\mu^{\pi}
-almost surely for any policy
\pi

(
Hutter 2009a
, Lem. 3i)
.

The stochastic process
w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

is a
\xi^{\pi_{T}}
-martingale according to
Section 3.3
.
By the martingale convergence theorem (
Section 2.2
)

w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})
converges
\xi^{\pi_{T}}
-almost surely and
because
\xi^{\pi_{T}}\geq w(\mu)\mu^{\pi_{T}}

it also converges
\mu^{\pi_{T}}
-almost surely.

We argue that we can choose
t_{0}

to be one of
\pi_{T}
’s resampling time steps large enough such that
for all
t\geq t_{0}

the following three events hold simultaneously
with
\mu^{\pi_{T}}
-probability at least
1-\delta
.

(i)

There is a finite set
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{M}
with

w(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})>1-\delta

and
w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k})\not\to 0
as
k\to\infty

for all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

\left|w(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-w(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}})\right|\leq\delta
for all
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

(iii)

F^{\pi_{T}}_{\infty}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})<\delta\beta w_{\min}^{2}
.

where

w_{\min}:=\inf\{w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<k})\mid k\in\mathbb{N},\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\}
,
which is positive by (
i
).

(
i
) and (
ii
) are satisfied eventually
because the posterior
w(\,\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

converges
\mu^{\pi_{T}}
-almost surely.
Note that the set
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is random:
the limit of
w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})
as
t\to\infty

depends on the history
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{1:\infty}
.
Without loss of generality,
we assume the true environment
\mu
is contained in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}

since
w(\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\not\to 0

\mu^{\pi_{T}}
-almost surely.
(
iii
) follows from
Section 5.4.3

since convergence in mean implies convergence in probability.

Moreover, we define the horizon
m:=t+H_{t}(\varepsilon_{t})

as the time step of
the effective horizon at time step
t
.
Let
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}
be a fixed history
for which (
i
-
iii
) is satisfied.
Then we have

\displaystyle\delta\beta w_{\min}^{2}

\displaystyle>F^{\pi_{T}}_{\infty}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}}w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})D_{\infty}(\nu^{\pi_{T}},\xi^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\nu\sim w(\,\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})}\left[D_{\infty}(\nu^{\pi_{T}},\xi^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\nu\sim w(\,\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})}\left[D_{m}(\nu^{\pi_{T}},\xi^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\beta w_{\min}^{2}w(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

by Markov’s inequality where

\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}:=\left\{\nu\in\mathcal{M}\;\middle|\;D_{m}(\nu^{\pi_{T}},\xi^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})<\beta w_{\min}^{2}\right\}.

For our fixed history
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}
we have

\displaystyle 1-\delta

\displaystyle<w(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{itm:M'})}}{{\leq}}w(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})+\delta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{itm:posterior-convergence})}}{{\leq}}w(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}})+2\delta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{itm:M'})}}{{\leq}}w(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}})+3\delta

and thus we get

1-4\delta<w\left(\{\nu\in\mathcal{M}\mid D_{m}(\nu^{\pi_{T}},\xi^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})<\beta w_{\min}^{2}\}\;\middle|\;\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}}\right).

(5.6)

In particular, this bound holds for
\nu=\mu

since
w(\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}})>4\delta
by assumption.

It remains to show that with high probability the value
V^{\pi^{*}_{\rho}}_{\mu}

of the sample
\rho
’s optimal policy
\pi^{*}_{\rho}
is sufficiently close to
the
\mu
-optimal value
V^{*}_{\mu}
.
The worst case is that we draw the worst sample from
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}

twice in a row.
From now on, let
\rho
denote the sample environment we draw at time step
t_{0}
,
and let
t
denote some time step between

t_{0}
and
t_{1}:=t_{0}+H_{t_{0}}(\varepsilon_{t_{0}})

(before the next resampling).
With probability
w(\nu^{\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}})w(\nu^{\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{1}})

we sample
\nu^{\prime}
both at
t_{0}
and
t_{1}

when following
\pi_{T}
.
Therefore we have for all
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{t:m}
and all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}

\nu^{\pi_{T}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{1:m}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\geq w(\nu^{\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}})w(\nu^{\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{1}})\nu^{\pi^{*}_{\nu^{\prime}}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{1:m}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}).

Thus we get for all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
(in particular
\rho
and
\mu
)

\displaystyle D_{m}(\mu^{\pi_{T}},\rho^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}}\sup_{A\subseteq(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E})^{m}}\Big|w(\nu^{\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}})w(\nu^{\prime}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{1}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad(\mu^{\pi^{*}_{\nu^{\prime}}}(A\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-\rho^{\pi^{*}_{\nu^{\prime}}}(A\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}))\Big|

\displaystyle\geq w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}})w(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{1}})\sup_{A\subseteq(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E})^{m}}\Big|\mu^{\pi^{*}_{\nu}}(A\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-\rho^{\pi^{*}_{\nu}}(A\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\Big|

\displaystyle\geq w_{\min}^{2}D_{m}(\mu^{\pi^{*}_{\nu}},\rho^{\pi^{*}_{\nu}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}).

For
\rho\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
we get with (
5.6
)

\displaystyle D_{m}(\mu^{\pi_{T}},\rho^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle\leq D_{m}(\mu^{\pi_{T}},\xi^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})+D_{m}(\rho^{\pi_{T}},\xi^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle<\beta w_{\min}^{2}+\beta w_{\min}^{2}=2\beta w_{\min}^{2},

which together with
Section 4.2.2

and the fact that rewards in
[0,1]
implies

\displaystyle\left|V^{\pi^{*}_{\nu}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi^{*}_{\nu}}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Gamma_{t+H_{t}(\varepsilon_{t})}}{\Gamma_{t}}+\left|V^{\pi^{*}_{\nu},m}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi^{*}_{\nu},m}_{\rho}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{t}+D_{m}(\mu^{\pi^{*}_{\nu}},\rho^{\pi^{*}_{\nu}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{t}+\tfrac{1}{w_{\min}^{2}}D_{m}(\mu^{\pi_{T}},\rho^{\pi_{T}}\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})

\displaystyle<\beta+2\beta=3\beta.

Hence we get (omitting history arguments
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}
for simplicity)

V^{*}_{\mu}=V^{\pi^{*}_{\mu}}_{\mu}<V^{\pi^{*}_{\mu}}_{\rho}+3\beta\leq V^{*}_{\rho}+3\beta=V^{\pi^{*}_{\rho}}_{\rho}+3\beta<V^{\pi^{*}_{\rho}}_{\mu}+3\beta+3\beta=V^{\pi^{*}_{\rho}}_{\mu}+6\beta.

(5.7)

With
\mu^{\pi_{T}}
-probability at least
1-\delta

(
i
), (
ii
), and (
iii
)
are true,
with
\mu^{\pi_{T}}
-probability at least
1-\delta

our sample
\rho
happens to be in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
by (
i
), and
with
w(\,\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t_{0}})
-probability at least
1-4\delta

the sample is in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
by (
5.6
).
All of these events are true simultaneously with probability at least

1-(\delta+\delta+4\delta)=1-6\delta
.
Hence the bound (
5.7
) transfers for
\pi_{T}
such that
with
\mu^{\pi_{T}}
-probability
\geq 1-6\delta
we have

V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi_{T}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})<6\beta.

Therefore

\mu^{\pi_{T}}[V^{*}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V^{\pi_{T}}_{\mu}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\geq 6\beta]<6\delta

and with
\delta\to 0
we get that

V_{\mu}^{*}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})-V_{\mu}^{\pi_{T}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\to 0
as
t\to\infty
in probability.
The value function is bounded, thus it also converges in mean.
∎

The following example shows that the Thompson sampling policy
is not strongly asymptotically optimal.
However, we expect that strong asymptotic optimality can be achieved
with Thompson sampling by resampling at every time step
(with strong assumptions on the discount function).
However, for practical purposes resampling in every time step
is very inefficient.

Example 5.28

(Thompson Sampling is not Strongly Asymptotically Optimal)
.

Define
\mathcal{A}:=\{\alpha,\beta\}
,
\mathcal{E}:=\{0,1/2,1\}
, and
assume geometric discounting (
Section 4.1.1
).
Consider the following class of environments

\mathcal{M}:=\{\nu_{\infty},\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots\}

(transitions are labeled with action, reward):

s_{0}
s_{1}
s_{2}
\beta,\frac{1}{2}
\alpha,0
\beta,0
\alpha,0
\ast,0

s_{0}
s_{1}
s_{2}
s_{3}
s_{4}
\beta,\frac{1}{2}
t<k:\alpha,0
\beta,0
\alpha,0
\ast,0
t\geq k:\alpha,0
\alpha,0
\beta,0
\alpha,1
\beta,0

\nu_{\infty}

\nu_{k}

Environment
\nu_{k}
works just like environment
\nu_{\infty}

except that after time step
k
, the path to state
s_{3}
gets unlocked.
The class
\mathcal{M}
is a class of deterministic weakly communicating POMDPs
(but as a POMDP
\nu_{k}
has more than 5 states).
The optimal policy in environment
\nu_{\infty}
is to always take action
\beta
,
the optimal policy for environment
\nu_{k}
is
to take action
\beta
for
t<k
and then
take action
\beta
in state
s_{1}
and action
\alpha
otherwise.

Suppose the policy
\pi_{T}
is acting in environment
\nu_{\infty}
.
Since it is asymptotically optimal in the class
\mathcal{M}
,
it has to take actions
\alpha\alpha
from
s_{0}
infinitely often:
for
t<k
environment
\nu_{k}
is indistinguishable from
\nu_{\infty}
,
so the posterior for
\nu_{k}
is larger or equal to the prior.
Hence there is always a constant chance of sampling
\nu_{k}

until taking actions
\alpha\alpha
,
at which point all environments
\nu_{k}
for
k\leq t
become falsified.

If the policy
\pi_{T}
decides to explore and take the first action
\alpha
,
it will be in state
s_{1}
.
Let
\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t}
denote the current history.
Then the
\nu_{\infty}
-optimal action is
\beta
and

V^{*}_{\nu_{\infty}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})=(1-\gamma)\left(0+\gamma\frac{1}{2}+\gamma^{2}\frac{1}{2}+\ldots\right)=\frac{\gamma}{2}.

The next action taken by
\pi_{T}
is
\alpha
since
any optimal policy for any sampled environment
that takes action
\alpha
once, takes that action again
(and we are following that policy for an
\varepsilon_{t}
-effective horizon).
Hence

V^{\pi_{T}}_{\nu_{\infty}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}{\mbox{\scriptsize{\ae}}}_{<t})\leq(1-\gamma)\left(0+0+\gamma^{2}\frac{1}{2}+\gamma^{3}\frac{1}{2}+\ldots\right)=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}.

Therefore
V^{*}_{\nu_{\infty}}-V^{\pi_{T}}_{\nu_{\infty}}\geq(\gamma-\gamma^{2})/2>0
.
This happens infinitely often with probability one and
thus we cannot get almost sure convergence.
∎

If the Bayesian mixture
\xi
is inside the class
\mathcal{M}

(as it is the case for the class
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{CCS}}_{\mathrm{LSC}}
),
then we can assign
\xi
a prior probability
that is arbitrarily close to
1
.
Since the posterior of
\xi
is the same as the prior,
Thompson sampling will act according to the Bayes optimal policy
most of the time.
This means the Bayes-value of Thompson sampling can be very good;
formally,

V^{*}_{\xi}(\epsilon)-V^{\pi_{T}}_{\xi}(\epsilon)=\overline{\Upsilon}_{\xi}-\Upsilon_{\xi}(\pi_{T})
can be made arbitrarily small.

In contrast, the Bayes-value of Thompson sampling can also be very bad:
Suppose you have a class of
(n+1)
-armed bandits indexed
1,\ldots,n

where bandit
i
gives reward
1-\varepsilon
on arm
1
, reward
1
on arm
i+1
,
and reward
0
on all other arms.
For geometric discounting
and
\varepsilon<(1-\gamma)/(2-\gamma)
,
it is Bayes optimal to pull arm
1
while Thompson sampling will explore on average

n/2
arms until it finds the optimal arm.
The Bayes-value of Thompson sampling is
1/(n-\gamma_{n-1})

in contract to
(1-\varepsilon)
achieved by Bayes.
For a horizon of
n
,
the Bayes optimal policy suffers a regret of
\varepsilon n
and
Thompson sampling a regret of
n/2
,
which is much larger for small
\varepsilon
.

5.4.4
Almost Sure in Cesàro Average vs. in Mean

It might appear that convergence in mean is more natural than
the convergence of Cesàro averages of weak asymptotic optimality.
However, both notions are not so fundamentally different
because they both allow an infinite number of bad mistakes
(actions that lead to
V^{*}_{\mu}-V^{\pi}_{\mu}
being large).
Asymptotic optimality in mean allows bad mistakes
as long as their probability converges to zero;
weak asymptotic optimality allows bad mistakes
as long as the total time spent on bad mistakes grows sublinearly.
Note that according to
Section 5.4

making bad mistakes infinitely often is necessary for asymptotic optimality.

Section 5.4.2
shows that weak asymptotic optimality is possible
in any countable class of stochastic environments.
However, this requires the additional condition that
the effective horizon grows sublinearly,
H_{t}(\varepsilon_{t})\in o(t)
,
while
Section 5.4.3

does not require any condition on the discount function.

Generally, weak asymptotic optimality and asymptotic optimality in mean
are incomparable
